3-Hour Exam Time Budget (20 Questions / 100 Marks Strategy)

Part A (10 Short Answers × 3 Marks = 30M): Allocate 5 mins per answer (50 mins total) — target ½ to 1 page highlighting key formula and core definition.
Part B (10 Essay Answers × 7 Marks = 70M): Allocate 12 mins per answer (120 mins total) — target 1.5 to 2 pages with schematic circuit/block diagrams and step-by-step derivations.
Strategy & Review Buffer (10 mins): 5 mins at start for question choice planning + 5 mins at end for question numbering and equation verification.

Course Code: EST130 • 5 Modules • 26 Questions

Basics of Electrical & Electronics Engineering

Essential electrical & electronic engineering: DC circuit theorems (KVL, KCL, Thevenin, Norton, Superposition), AC single-phase & three-phase star/delta systems, magnetic circuits, transformers and DC/AC machines, PN junction diodes, BJT amplifier configurations, operational amplifiers, and digital logic gates.

Module 1

DC Circuits & Network Theorems

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Short Answer ID: BEEE-Q1

State and explain Kirchhoff's Current Law (KCL) and Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) with algebraic sign conventions. Explain how they form the basis of mesh and nodal analysis. State and explain Kirchhoff's Current Law (KCL) and Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) with algebraic sign conventions. Explain how they form the basis of mesh and nodal analysis. State and explain Kirchhoff's Current Law (KCL) and Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) with algebraic sign conventions. Explain how they form the basis of mesh and nodal analysis.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\sum I_{\text{in}} = \sum I_{\text{out}}, \quad \sum_{k=1}^m V_k = 0$$

Kirchhoff’s Current Law (KCL): Conservation of electric charge; algebraic sum of currents entering and leaving any node is zero ($\sum I = 0$).
Kirchhoff’s Voltage Law (KVL): Conservation of energy; algebraic sum of potential rises and drops around any closed loop is zero ($\sum V = 0$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\sum I_{\text{in}} = \sum I_{\text{out}}, \quad \sum_{k=1}^m V_k = 0$$

Kirchhoff’s Current Law (KCL): Conservation of electric charge; algebraic sum of currents entering and leaving any node is zero ($\sum I = 0$).
Kirchhoff’s Voltage Law (KVL): Conservation of energy; algebraic sum of potential rises and drops around any closed loop is zero ($\sum V = 0$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\sum I_{\text{in}} = \sum I_{\text{out}}, \quad \sum_{k=1}^m V_k = 0$$

Kirchhoff’s Current Law (KCL): Conservation of electric charge; algebraic sum of currents entering and leaving any node is zero ($\sum I = 0$).
Kirchhoff’s Voltage Law (KVL): Conservation of energy; algebraic sum of potential rises and drops around any closed loop is zero ($\sum V = 0$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Kirchhoff's laws form the bedrock of electric circuit analysis, derived directly from the fundamental conservation laws of classical physics. Kirchhoff's Current Law (KCL) is a direct consequence of the principle of conservation of electric charge. It states that the algebraic sum of all electric currents entering and leaving any node (or junction) in an electrical network is identically zero at every instant of time: $$\sum_{k=1}^{n} I_k = 0$$ In applying KCL, the standard sign convention designates currents directed toward the node as positive ($+I$) and currents directed away from the node as negative ($-I$), or equivalently, the total incoming current equals the total outgoing current: $$\sum I_{\text{in}} = \sum I_{\text{out}}$$ Because an electrical node cannot accumulate or store electric charge over time, any charge arriving at a junction must immediately depart through the connected branches, guaranteeing strict charge conservation.

Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) is the circuit-theoretic expression of the law of conservation of energy. It states that the algebraic sum of all branch voltages (electromotive forces and potential differences across passive elements) around any closed loop or mesh in an electric network is identically zero: $$\sum_{k=1}^{m} V_k = 0$$ When tracing a closed path clockwise or counterclockwise, a consistent potential sign convention must be maintained. Traversing an element from its negative terminal to its positive terminal represents a potential rise, which is assigned a positive algebraic sign ($+V$). Conversely, traversing from a positive terminal to a negative terminal corresponds to a potential drop, which is assigned a negative algebraic sign ($-V$). Across an ideal resistor of resistance $R$ carrying branch current $I$, the potential drop in the direction of assumed current flow is $-I R$, while traversing against the assumed current represents a potential rise of $+I R$.

In systematic circuit analysis, KVL forms the mathematical foundation of Mesh Current Analysis, where closed planar loops are assigned circulating mesh currents, generating a set of simultaneous linear algebraic equations in terms of loop impedances and source voltages: $$\mathbf{R} \cdot \mathbf{I} = \mathbf{V}$$ Conversely, KCL forms the mathematical foundation of Nodal Voltage Analysis, where one node is designated as the reference datum (ground at $0\text{ V}$) and KCL equations are written at all remaining non-reference nodes in terms of node voltages and branch conductances: $$\mathbf{G} \cdot \mathbf{V} = \mathbf{I}$$ Together with Ohm's law ($V = I R$), these two topological conservation laws allow the complete determination of all branch voltages, currents, and power distributions in any linear bilateral DC network.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Numerical Problem ID: BEEE-Q2

Explain Nodal Voltage Analysis. Explain the supernode concept when an ideal independent voltage source is connected between two non-reference nodes, and solve a numerical problem. Explain Nodal Voltage Analysis. Explain the supernode concept when an ideal independent voltage source is connected between two non-reference nodes, and solve a numerical problem. Explain Nodal Voltage Analysis. Explain the supernode concept when an ideal independent voltage source is connected between two non-reference nodes, and solve a numerical problem.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_1 - V_2 = V_s, \quad \sum I_{\text{supernode}} = 0$$

Supernode: Formed when an ideal voltage source is connected between two non-reference nodes. Replace the source branch with an enclosed boundary and apply KCL to the combined supernode, augmented by the source constraint equation ($V_1 - V_2 = V_s$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_1 - V_2 = V_s, \quad \sum I_{\text{supernode}} = 0$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_1 - V_2 = V_s, \quad \sum I_{\text{supernode}} = 0$$

Supernode: Formed when an ideal voltage source is connected between two non-reference nodes. Replace the source branch with an enclosed boundary and apply KCL to the combined supernode, augmented by the source constraint equation ($V_1 - V_2 = V_s$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Nodal voltage analysis provides an algorithmic, computationally efficient method to solve for all branch voltages and currents by expressing Kirchhoff's Current Law in terms of node potentials. In a circuit with $N$ principal nodes, one node is selected as the common reference datum ($0\text{ V}$), and the remaining $(N-1)$ node voltages are defined with respect to this datum. When an ideal independent or dependent voltage source is connected between two non-reference nodes, the current flowing through that voltage source branch cannot be determined directly from Ohm's law. To circumvent this singularity, the two nodes connected by the voltage source, along with the source itself, are enclosed within a generalized boundary called a Supernode.

To illustrate this method completely with rigorous step-by-step working, consider a standard university examination network consisting of three essential nodes plus a ground reference node. Let Node 0 be the ground reference ($V_0 = 0\text{ V}$). An independent voltage source of $V_s = 20\text{ V}$ is connected between Node 1 and Node 2 such that $V_1 - V_2 = 20\text{ V}$. An independent current source of $I_s = 5\text{ A}$ enters Node 1. Resistors are connected as follows: $R_1 = 4\,\Omega$ from Node 1 to ground, $R_2 = 2\,\Omega$ from Node 2 to Node 3, $R_3 = 8\,\Omega$ from Node 2 to ground, and $R_4 = 4\,\Omega$ from Node 3 to ground. A constant current source of $2\text{ A}$ is directed out of Node 3 into ground.

Step 1: Constraint Equation for the Supernode (formed by Node 1 and Node 2): The independent $20\text{ V}$ source directly fixes the potential difference between Node 1 and Node 2: $$V_1 - V_2 = 20 \implies V_1 = V_2 + 20$$

Step 2: KCL Equation at the Supernode (Nodes 1 and 2 combined): Summing all currents leaving the supernode boundary: $$\frac{V_1 - 0}{R_1} - 5 + \frac{V_2 - 0}{R_3} + \frac{V_2 - V_3}{R_2} = 0$$ Substituting the numerical component values $R_1 = 4\,\Omega$, $R_2 = 2\,\Omega$, and $R_3 = 8\,\Omega$: $$\frac{V_1}{4} - 5 + \frac{V_2}{8} + \frac{V_2 - V_3}{2} = 0$$ Multiplying the entire equation by the common denominator $8$: $$2 V_1 - 40 + V_2 + 4(V_2 - V_3) = 0 \implies 2 V_1 + 5 V_2 - 4 V_3 = 40$$ Substituting the constraint equation $V_1 = V_2 + 20$: $$2(V_2 + 20) + 5 V_2 - 4 V_3 = 40 \implies 2 V_2 + 40 + 5 V_2 - 4 V_3 = 40$$ $$7 V_2 - 4 V_3 = 0 \implies V_3 = \frac{7}{4} V_2 = 1.75 V_2$$

Step 3: KCL Equation at Node 3: Summing all currents leaving Node 3: $$\frac{V_3 - V_2}{R_2} + \frac{V_3 - 0}{R_4} + 2 = 0$$ Substituting $R_2 = 2\,\Omega$ and $R_4 = 4\,\Omega$: $$\frac{V_3 - V_2}{2} + \frac{V_3}{4} + 2 = 0$$ Multiplying through by $4$: $$2(V_3 - V_2) + V_3 + 8 = 0 \implies -2 V_2 + 3 V_3 = -8$$

Step 4: Solving for Node Voltages: Substitute $V_3 = 1.75 V_2$ into the Node 3 equation: $$-2 V_2 + 3(1.75 V_2) = -8 \implies -2 V_2 + 5.25 V_2 = -8$$ $$3.25 V_2 = -8 \implies V_2 = -\frac{8}{3.25} = -\frac{32}{13} \approx -2.4615\text{ V}$$ Now calculate $V_1$ using the supernode constraint: $$V_1 = V_2 + 20 = -2.4615 + 20 = +17.5385\text{ V}$$ Now calculate $V_3$: $$V_3 = 1.75 V_2 = 1.75(-2.4615) = -4.3077\text{ V}$$

Step 5: Determining Branch Currents: The current through the $4\,\Omega$ resistor connected to Node 1 is: $$I_{R1} = \frac{V_1}{4} = \frac{17.5385}{4} = 4.3846\text{ A}$$ The current flowing from Node 2 to Node 3 through the $2\,\Omega$ resistor is: $$I_{R2} = \frac{V_2 - V_3}{R_2} = \frac{-2.4615 - (-4.3077)}{2} = \frac{1.8462}{2} = 0.9231\text{ A}$$ The current through the $8\,\Omega$ resistor connected to Node 2 is: $$I_{R3} = \frac{V_2}{8} = \frac{-2.4615}{8} = -0.3077\text{ A}$$ This rigorous calculation confirms that all node potentials and branch currents are uniquely determined by combining KCL with the supernode constraint.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Numerical Problem ID: BEEE-Q3

State Superposition Theorem. Explain its conditions of applicability and limitations. Find the current through the load resistor in a multi-source DC network with independent voltage and current sources. State Superposition Theorem. Explain its conditions of applicability and limitations. Find the current through the load resistor in a multi-source DC network with independent voltage and current sources. State Superposition Theorem. Explain its conditions of applicability and limitations. Find the current through the load resistor in a multi-source DC network with independent voltage and current sources.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_L = I_L' + I_L'' + I_L''' + \dots = \sum_{k=1}^n I_L^{(k)}$$

Superposition Theorem: In any linear bilateral network with multiple independent sources, the response in any branch is the algebraic sum of responses caused by each independent source acting alone, with all other voltage sources shorted ($V=0$) and current sources opened ($I=0$). Does not apply directly to power.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_L = I_L' + I_L'' + I_L''' + \dots = \sum_{k=1}^n I_L^{(k)}$$

Superposition Theorem: In any linear bilateral network with multiple independent sources, the response in any branch is the algebraic sum of responses caused by each independent source acting alone, with all other voltage sources shorted ($V=0$) and current sources opened ($I=0$). Does not apply directly to power.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_L = I_L' + I_L'' + I_L''' + \dots = \sum_{k=1}^n I_L^{(k)}$$

Superposition Theorem: In any linear bilateral network with multiple independent sources, the response in any branch is the algebraic sum of responses caused by each independent source acting alone, with all other voltage sources shorted ($V=0$) and current sources opened ($I=0$). Does not apply directly to power.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Superposition Theorem is a direct manifestation of mathematical linearity in electric networks containing bilateral linear circuit elements (resistors, inductors, and capacitors) and independent energy sources. The theorem states: In any linear bilateral electric network energized by two or more independent sources, the resultant voltage across or current through any circuit element is equal to the algebraic sum of the individual voltages or currents produced by each independent source acting alone, with all other independent sources deactivated (turned off).

To deactivate independent sources: 1. An ideal independent voltage source is deactivated by reducing its terminal potential difference to zero, which corresponds to replacing it with a short circuit ($V_s = 0\text{ V}$). 2. An ideal independent current source is deactivated by reducing its branch current to zero, which corresponds to replacing it with an open circuit ($I_s = 0\text{ A}$). Dependent (controlled) sources cannot be deactivated; their control dependencies must remain fully active in every sub-circuit analysis. Furthermore, the superposition theorem applies strictly to linear quantities (voltages and currents) and cannot be applied directly to calculate power ($P = I^2 R$ or $P = V^2 / R$) because power is a quadratic, non-linear function of electrical state variables.

To demonstrate the full mathematical execution of the Superposition Theorem, consider the classic KTU university problem: Determine the current $I_L$ flowing from Node A to Node B through a load resistor $R_L = 6\,\Omega$ connected in a network energized simultaneously by a $V_1 = 30\text{ V}$ DC voltage source, a $V_2 = 18\text{ V}$ DC voltage source, and an independent current source $I_s = 3\text{ A}$. The circuit topology is as follows: Source $V_1 = 30\text{ V}$ is in series with resistor $R_1 = 3\,\Omega$ connected to Node A. Source $V_2 = 18\text{ V}$ is in series with resistor $R_2 = 6\,\Omega$ connected to Node A. Current source $I_s = 3\text{ A}$ is connected in parallel across the branch between Node A and Node B, directed into Node A. Resistor $R_L = 6\,\Omega$ is connected between Node A and Node B, and Node B is connected to the common ground reference ($0\text{ V}$).

Step 1: Response due to $V_1 = 30\text{ V}$ acting alone: Deactivate $V_2$ (short circuit) and deactivate $I_s$ (open circuit). At Node A, resistor $R_1 = 3\,\Omega$ connects to the $30\text{ V}$ source. The other two parallel paths connected from Node A to ground are $R_2 = 6\,\Omega$ and $R_L = 6\,\Omega$. The equivalent parallel resistance of $R_2$ and $R_L$ is: $$R_{p1} = \frac{R_2 \cdot R_L}{R_2 + R_L} = \frac{6 \times 6}{6 + 6} = \frac{36}{12} = 3\,\Omega$$ The total equivalent resistance seen by the $30\text{ V}$ source is: $$R_{eq1} = R_1 + R_{p1} = 3 + 3 = 6\,\Omega$$ The total current supplied by the $30\text{ V}$ source into Node A is: $$I_{total1} = \frac{V_1}{R_{eq1}} = \frac{30}{6} = 5\text{ A}$$ By current division between identical parallel resistors $R_2 = 6\,\Omega$ and $R_L = 6\,\Omega$, the contribution of $V_1$ to the load current is: $$I_L' = I_{total1} \left( \frac{R_2}{R_2 + R_L} \right) = 5 \times \frac{6}{6 + 6} = 5 \times 0.5 = 2.5\text{ A (flowing downwards)}$$

Step 2: Response due to $V_2 = 18\text{ V}$ acting alone: Deactivate $V_1$ (short circuit) and deactivate $I_s$ (open circuit). At Node A, resistor $R_2 = 6\,\Omega$ connects to the $18\text{ V}$ source. The parallel paths from Node A to ground are $R_1 = 3\,\Omega$ and $R_L = 6\,\Omega$. The equivalent parallel resistance of $R_1$ and $R_L$ is: $$R_{p2} = \frac{R_1 \cdot R_L}{R_1 + R_L} = \frac{3 \times 6}{3 + 6} = \frac{18}{9} = 2\,\Omega$$ The total resistance seen by the $18\text{ V}$ source is: $$R_{eq2} = R_2 + R_{p2} = 6 + 2 = 8\,\Omega$$ The total current supplied by source $V_2$ into Node A is: $$I_{total2} = \frac{V_2}{R_{eq2}} = \frac{18}{8} = 2.25\text{ A}$$ By current division between $R_1 = 3\,\Omega$ and $R_L = 6\,\Omega$, the contribution of $V_2$ to the load current is: $$I_L'' = I_{total2} \left( \frac{R_1}{R_1 + R_L} \right) = 2.25 \times \frac{3}{3 + 6} = 2.25 \times \frac{1}{3} = 0.75\text{ A (flowing downwards)}$$

Step 3: Response due to $I_s = 3\text{ A}$ acting alone: Deactivate $V_1$ (short circuit to ground) and deactivate $V_2$ (short circuit to ground). All three resistors $R_1 = 3\,\Omega$, $R_2 = 6\,\Omega$, and $R_L = 6\,\Omega$ are connected in parallel between Node A and Node B (ground). The equivalent resistance of $R_1$ and $R_2$ in parallel is: $$R_{12} = \frac{R_1 \cdot R_2}{R_1 + R_2} = \frac{3 \times 6}{3 + 6} = 2\,\Omega$$ The current source $I_s = 3\text{ A}$ pumps current directly into Node A, which divides between the parallel branch $R_{12} = 2\,\Omega$ and the load branch $R_L = 6\,\Omega$. By current division, the current through $R_L$ is: $$I_L''' = I_s \left( \frac{R_{12}}{R_{12} + R_L} \right) = 3 \times \frac{2}{2 + 6} = 3 \times \frac{2}{8} = 3 \times 0.25 = 0.75\text{ A (flowing downwards)}$$

Step 4: Algebraic Summation of Individual Responses: Because all three component currents $I_L'$, $I_L''$, and $I_L'''$ flow in the identical direction (downwards from Node A to Node B): $$I_L = I_L' + I_L'' + I_L''' = 2.5\text{ A} + 0.75\text{ A} + 0.75\text{ A} = 4.0\text{ A}$$ The final total load current flowing through $R_L = 6\,\Omega$ is exactly: $$I_L = 4.0\text{ A}$$

Step 2: Response due to $V_2 = 18\text{ V}$ acting alone: Deactivate $V_1$ (short circuit) and deactivate $I_s$ (open circuit). At Node A, resistor $R_2 = 6\,\Omega$ connects to the $18\text{ V}$ source. The parallel paths from Node A to ground are $R_1 = 3\,\Omega$ and $R_L = 6\,\Omega$. The equivalent parallel resistance of $R_1$ and $R_L$ is: $$R_{p2} = \frac{R_1 \cdot R_L}{R_1 + R_L} = \frac{3 \times 6}{3 + 6} = \frac{18}{9} = 2\,\Omega$$ The total resistance seen by the $18\text{ V}$ source is: $$R_{eq2} = R_2 + R_{p2} = 6 + 2 = 8\,\Omega$$ The total current supplied by source $V_2$ into Node A is: $$I_{total2} = \frac{V_2}{R_{eq2}} = \frac{18}{8} = 2.25\text{ A}$$ By current division between $R_1 = 3\,\Omega$ and $R_L = 6\,\Omega$, the contribution of $V_2$ to the load current is: $$I_L'' = I_{total2} \left( \frac{R_1}{R_1 + R_L} \right) = 2.25 \times \frac{3}{3 + 6} = 2.25 \times \frac{1}{3} = 0.75\text{ A (flowing downwards)}$$

Step 3: Response due to $I_s = 3\text{ A}$ acting alone: Deactivate $V_1$ (short circuit to ground) and deactivate $V_2$ (short circuit to ground). All three resistors $R_1 = 3\,\Omega$, $R_2 = 6\,\Omega$, and $R_L = 6\,\Omega$ are connected in parallel between Node A and Node B (ground). The equivalent resistance of $R_1$ and $R_2$ in parallel is: $$R_{12} = \frac{R_1 \cdot R_2}{R_1 + R_2} = \frac{3 \times 6}{3 + 6} = 2\,\Omega$$ The current source $I_s = 3\text{ A}$ pumps current directly into Node A, which divides between the parallel branch $R_{12} = 2\,\Omega$ and the load branch $R_L = 6\,\Omega$. By current division, the current through $R_L$ is: $$I_L''' = I_s \left( \frac{R_{12}}{R_{12} + R_L} \right) = 3 \times \frac{2}{2 + 6} = 3 \times \frac{2}{8} = 3 \times 0.25 = 0.75\text{ A (flowing downwards)}$$

Step 4: Algebraic Summation of Individual Responses: Because all three component currents $I_L'$, $I_L''$, and $I_L'''$ flow in the identical direction (downwards from Node A to Node B): $$I_L = I_L' + I_L'' + I_L''' = 2.5\text{ A} + 0.75\text{ A} + 0.75\text{ A} = 4.0\text{ A}$$ The final total load current flowing through $R_L = 6\,\Omega$ is exactly: $$I_L = 4.0\text{ A}$$

Numerical Problem ID: BEEE-Q4

State Thevenin's and Norton's Theorems and derive their dual relationship. Find the Thevenin and Norton equivalent circuits across the detector terminals of an unbalanced bridge network. State Thevenin's and Norton's Theorems and derive their dual relationship. Find the Thevenin and Norton equivalent circuits across the detector terminals of an unbalanced bridge network. State Thevenin's and Norton's Theorems and derive their dual relationship. Find the Thevenin and Norton equivalent circuits across the detector terminals of an unbalanced bridge network.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{th} = V_{oc}, \quad R_{th} = R_N, \quad I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}}, \quad I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} = I_N \left(\frac{R_N}{R_N + R_L}\right)$$

Thevenin’s Theorem: Replaces network across load terminals with open-circuit voltage $V_{th}$ in series with $R_{th}$.
Norton’s Theorem: Replaces network with short-circuit current $I_N$ in parallel with $R_N = R_{th}$. Dual relationship: $V_{th} = I_N R_{th}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{th} = V_{oc}, \quad R_{th} = R_N, \quad I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}}, \quad I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} = I_N \left(\frac{R_N}{R_N + R_L}\right)$$

Thevenin’s Theorem: Replaces network across load terminals with open-circuit voltage $V_{th}$ in series with $R_{th}$.
Norton’s Theorem: Replaces network with short-circuit current $I_N$ in parallel with $R_N = R_{th}$. Dual relationship: $V_{th} = I_N R_{th}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{th} = V_{oc}, \quad R_{th} = R_N, \quad I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}}, \quad I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} = I_N \left(\frac{R_N}{R_N + R_L}\right)$$

Thevenin’s Theorem: Replaces network across load terminals with open-circuit voltage $V_{th}$ in series with $R_{th}$.
Norton’s Theorem: Replaces network with short-circuit current $I_N$ in parallel with $R_N = R_{th}$. Dual relationship: $V_{th} = I_N R_{th}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Thevenin's and Norton's theorems provide powerful network reduction techniques that transform any complex, multi-source linear bilateral active network into an ultra-simple two-terminal equivalent circuit.

Thevenin's Theorem states: Any linear bilateral active two-terminal network containing resistance elements and energy sources can be completely replaced, as viewed from its load terminals A-B, by an equivalent circuit consisting of a single independent voltage source $V_{th}$ connected in series with an equivalent resistance $R_{th}$. Here, $V_{th}$ (Thevenin voltage) is the open-circuit potential difference measured across terminals A-B when the load resistor is disconnected ($V_{th} = V_{oc}$). $R_{th}$ (Thevenin resistance) is the equivalent input resistance seen looking back into terminals A-B with the load removed and all independent energy sources deactivated (voltage sources replaced by short circuits, current sources replaced by open circuits).

Norton's Theorem is the exact source-transformation dual of Thevenin's theorem. It states: Any linear bilateral active two-terminal network can be replaced, across terminals A-B, by an equivalent circuit consisting of a single independent current source $I_N$ connected in parallel with an equivalent resistance $R_N$. Here, $I_N$ (Norton current) is the short-circuit current that flows when terminals A-B are bridged by a zero-resistance conductor ($I_N = I_{sc}$). The Norton equivalent resistance is identical to the Thevenin equivalent resistance: $$R_N = R_{th}$$ The algebraic bridge linking both theorems is Ohm's law: $$V_{th} = I_N \cdot R_{th} \iff I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}}$$

To illustrate the complete procedure, consider an unbalanced Wheatstone bridge network energized by a DC voltage source $V_s = 100\text{ V}$. The bridge branches are: Arm AC has $R_1 = 20\,\Omega$, Arm CB has $R_2 = 30\,\Omega$, Arm AD has $R_3 = 40\,\Omega$, and Arm DB has $R_4 = 10\,\Omega$. The source $100\text{ V}$ is connected across diagonal terminals A and B. A galvanometer or load resistor $R_L = 15\,\Omega$ is connected across the detector terminals C and D. We wish to determine the Thevenin and Norton equivalent circuits across terminals C-D, and calculate the current flowing through $R_L$.

Step 1: Determination of Thevenin Voltage $V_{th} = V_{CD(oc)}$: Disconnect load resistor $R_L$ between terminals C and D. Because terminals C and D are open-circuited, branch ACB and branch ADB act as two isolated parallel voltage dividers connected directly across the $100\text{ V}$ supply. The potential of node C with respect to reference terminal B is: $$V_C = V_s \left( \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} \right) = 100 \times \left( \frac{30}{20 + 30} \right) = 100 \times \frac{30}{50} = 60\text{ V}$$ The potential of node D with respect to reference terminal B is: $$V_D = V_s \left( \frac{R_4}{R_3 + R_4} \right) = 100 \times \left( \frac{10}{40 + 10} \right) = 100 \times \frac{10}{50} = 20\text{ V}$$ The open-circuit Thevenin voltage between terminals C and D is: $$V_{th} = V_C - V_D = 60\text{ V} - 20\text{ V} = +40\text{ V}$$ with terminal C positive with respect to terminal D.

Step 2: Determination of Thevenin Resistance $R_{th}$: Deactivate the independent voltage source $V_s = 100\text{ V}$ by replacing it with a short circuit between terminal A and terminal B. Looking into terminals C and D: Arm AC ($R_1 = 20\,\Omega$) and Arm CB ($R_2 = 30\,\Omega$) are now connected in parallel between terminal C and the common node A-B: $$R_{p1} = \frac{R_1 \cdot R_2}{R_1 + R_2} = \frac{20 \times 30}{20 + 30} = \frac{600}{50} = 12\,\Omega$$ Similarly, Arm AD ($R_3 = 40\,\Omega$) and Arm DB ($R_4 = 10\,\Omega$) are connected in parallel between terminal D and the common node A-B: $$R_{p2} = \frac{R_3 \cdot R_4}{R_3 + R_4} = \frac{40 \times 10}{40 + 10} = \frac{400}{50} = 8\,\Omega$$ The total Thevenin equivalent resistance between terminals C and D is the series combination of these two parallel pairs: $$R_{th} = R_{p1} + R_{p2} = 12\,\Omega + 8\,\Omega = 20\,\Omega$$

Step 3: Norton Equivalent Representation: The Norton equivalent resistance is: $$R_N = R_{th} = 20\,\Omega$$ The Norton short-circuit current is: $$I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}} = \frac{40\text{ V}}{20\,\Omega} = 2.0\text{ A}$$

Step 4: Load Current and Power Calculation: Reconnecting the load resistor $R_L = 15\,\Omega$ across the Thevenin equivalent terminals C-D: $$I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} = \frac{40}{20 + 15} = \frac{40}{35} = \frac{8}{7} \approx 1.1429\text{ A}$$ Using the Norton equivalent circuit with current division: $$I_L = I_N \left( \frac{R_N}{R_N + R_L} \right) = 2.0 \times \left( \frac{20}{20 + 15} \right) = 2.0 \times \frac{20}{35} = \frac{40}{35} \approx 1.1429\text{ A}$$ Both equivalent models yield identical physical results, confirming the theorem.

The Norton equivalent resistance is identical to the Thevenin equivalent resistance: $$R_N = R_{th}$$ The algebraic bridge linking both theorems is Ohm's law: $$V_{th} = I_N \cdot R_{th} \iff I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}}$$

To illustrate the complete procedure, consider an unbalanced Wheatstone bridge network energized by a DC voltage source $V_s = 100\text{ V}$. The bridge branches are: Arm AC has $R_1 = 20\,\Omega$, Arm CB has $R_2 = 30\,\Omega$, Arm AD has $R_3 = 40\,\Omega$, and Arm DB has $R_4 = 10\,\Omega$. The source $100\text{ V}$ is connected across diagonal terminals A and B. A galvanometer or load resistor $R_L = 15\,\Omega$ is connected across the detector terminals C and D. We wish to determine the Thevenin and Norton equivalent circuits across terminals C-D, and calculate the current flowing through $R_L$.

Step 1: Determination of Thevenin Voltage $V_{th} = V_{CD(oc)}$: Disconnect load resistor $R_L$ between terminals C and D. Because terminals C and D are open-circuited, branch ACB and branch ADB act as two isolated parallel voltage dividers connected directly across the $100\text{ V}$ supply. The potential of node C with respect to reference terminal B is: $$V_C = V_s \left( \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} \right) = 100 \times \left( \frac{30}{20 + 30} \right) = 100 \times \frac{30}{50} = 60\text{ V}$$ The potential of node D with respect to reference terminal B is: $$V_D = V_s \left( \frac{R_4}{R_3 + R_4} \right) = 100 \times \left( \frac{10}{40 + 10} \right) = 100 \times \frac{10}{50} = 20\text{ V}$$ The open-circuit Thevenin voltage between terminals C and D is: $$V_{th} = V_C - V_D = 60\text{ V} - 20\text{ V} = +40\text{ V}$$ with terminal C positive with respect to terminal D.

Step 2: Determination of Thevenin Resistance $R_{th}$: Deactivate the independent voltage source $V_s = 100\text{ V}$ by replacing it with a short circuit between terminal A and terminal B. Looking into terminals C and D: Arm AC ($R_1 = 20\,\Omega$) and Arm CB ($R_2 = 30\,\Omega$) are now connected in parallel between terminal C and the common node A-B: $$R_{p1} = \frac{R_1 \cdot R_2}{R_1 + R_2} = \frac{20 \times 30}{20 + 30} = \frac{600}{50} = 12\,\Omega$$ Similarly, Arm AD ($R_3 = 40\,\Omega$) and Arm DB ($R_4 = 10\,\Omega$) are connected in parallel between terminal D and the common node A-B: $$R_{p2} = \frac{R_3 \cdot R_4}{R_3 + R_4} = \frac{40 \times 10}{40 + 10} = \frac{400}{50} = 8\,\Omega$$ The total Thevenin equivalent resistance between terminals C and D is the series combination of these two parallel pairs: $$R_{th} = R_{p1} + R_{p2} = 12\,\Omega + 8\,\Omega = 20\,\Omega$$

Step 3: Norton Equivalent Representation: The Norton equivalent resistance is: $$R_N = R_{th} = 20\,\Omega$$ The Norton short-circuit current is: $$I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}} = \frac{40\text{ V}}{20\,\Omega} = 2.0\text{ A}$$

Step 4: Load Current and Power Calculation: Reconnecting the load resistor $R_L = 15\,\Omega$ across the Thevenin equivalent terminals C-D: $$I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} = \frac{40}{20 + 15} = \frac{40}{35} = \frac{8}{7} \approx 1.1429\text{ A}$$ Using the Norton equivalent circuit with current division: $$I_L = I_N \left( \frac{R_N}{R_N + R_L} \right) = 2.0 \times \left( \frac{20}{20 + 15} \right) = 2.0 \times \frac{20}{35} = \frac{40}{35} \approx 1.1429\text{ A}$$ Both equivalent models yield identical physical results, confirming the theorem.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Thevenin's and Norton's theorems provide powerful network reduction techniques that transform any complex, multi-source linear bilateral active network into an ultra-simple two-terminal equivalent circuit.

Thevenin's Theorem states: Any linear bilateral active two-terminal network containing resistance elements and energy sources can be completely replaced, as viewed from its load terminals A-B, by an equivalent circuit consisting of a single independent voltage source $V_{th}$ connected in series with an equivalent resistance $R_{th}$. Here, $V_{th}$ (Thevenin voltage) is the open-circuit potential difference measured across terminals A-B when the load resistor is disconnected ($V_{th} = V_{oc}$). $R_{th}$ (Thevenin resistance) is the equivalent input resistance seen looking back into terminals A-B with the load removed and all independent energy sources deactivated (voltage sources replaced by short circuits, current sources replaced by open circuits).

Norton's Theorem is the exact source-transformation dual of Thevenin's theorem. It states: Any linear bilateral active two-terminal network can be replaced, across terminals A-B, by an equivalent circuit consisting of a single independent current source $I_N$ connected in parallel with an equivalent resistance $R_N$. Here, $I_N$ (Norton current) is the short-circuit current that flows when terminals A-B are bridged by a zero-resistance conductor ($I_N = I_{sc}$). The Norton equivalent resistance is identical to the Thevenin equivalent resistance: $$R_N = R_{th}$$ The algebraic bridge linking both theorems is Ohm's law: $$V_{th} = I_N \cdot R_{th} \iff I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}}$$

To illustrate the complete procedure, consider an unbalanced Wheatstone bridge network energized by a DC voltage source $V_s = 100\text{ V}$. The bridge branches are: Arm AC has $R_1 = 20\,\Omega$, Arm CB has $R_2 = 30\,\Omega$, Arm AD has $R_3 = 40\,\Omega$, and Arm DB has $R_4 = 10\,\Omega$. The source $100\text{ V}$ is connected across diagonal terminals A and B. A galvanometer or load resistor $R_L = 15\,\Omega$ is connected across the detector terminals C and D. We wish to determine the Thevenin and Norton equivalent circuits across terminals C-D, and calculate the current flowing through $R_L$.

Step 1: Determination of Thevenin Voltage $V_{th} = V_{CD(oc)}$: Disconnect load resistor $R_L$ between terminals C and D. Because terminals C and D are open-circuited, branch ACB and branch ADB act as two isolated parallel voltage dividers connected directly across the $100\text{ V}$ supply. The potential of node C with respect to reference terminal B is: $$V_C = V_s \left( \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} \right) = 100 \times \left( \frac{30}{20 + 30} \right) = 100 \times \frac{30}{50} = 60\text{ V}$$ The potential of node D with respect to reference terminal B is: $$V_D = V_s \left( \frac{R_4}{R_3 + R_4} \right) = 100 \times \left( \frac{10}{40 + 10} \right) = 100 \times \frac{10}{50} = 20\text{ V}$$ The open-circuit Thevenin voltage between terminals C and D is: $$V_{th} = V_C - V_D = 60\text{ V} - 20\text{ V} = +40\text{ V}$$ with terminal C positive with respect to terminal D.

Step 2: Determination of Thevenin Resistance $R_{th}$: Deactivate the independent voltage source $V_s = 100\text{ V}$ by replacing it with a short circuit between terminal A and terminal B. Looking into terminals C and D: Arm AC ($R_1 = 20\,\Omega$) and Arm CB ($R_2 = 30\,\Omega$) are now connected in parallel between terminal C and the common node A-B: $$R_{p1} = \frac{R_1 \cdot R_2}{R_1 + R_2} = \frac{20 \times 30}{20 + 30} = \frac{600}{50} = 12\,\Omega$$ Similarly, Arm AD ($R_3 = 40\,\Omega$) and Arm DB ($R_4 = 10\,\Omega$) are connected in parallel between terminal D and the common node A-B: $$R_{p2} = \frac{R_3 \cdot R_4}{R_3 + R_4} = \frac{40 \times 10}{40 + 10} = \frac{400}{50} = 8\,\Omega$$ The total Thevenin equivalent resistance between terminals C and D is the series combination of these two parallel pairs: $$R_{th} = R_{p1} + R_{p2} = 12\,\Omega + 8\,\Omega = 20\,\Omega$$

Step 3: Norton Equivalent Representation: The Norton equivalent resistance is: $$R_N = R_{th} = 20\,\Omega$$ The Norton short-circuit current is: $$I_N = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th}} = \frac{40\text{ V}}{20\,\Omega} = 2.0\text{ A}$$

Step 4: Load Current and Power Calculation: Reconnecting the load resistor $R_L = 15\,\Omega$ across the Thevenin equivalent terminals C-D: $$I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} = \frac{40}{20 + 15} = \frac{40}{35} = \frac{8}{7} \approx 1.1429\text{ A}$$ Using the Norton equivalent circuit with current division: $$I_L = I_N \left( \frac{R_N}{R_N + R_L} \right) = 2.0 \times \left( \frac{20}{20 + 15} \right) = 2.0 \times \frac{20}{35} = \frac{40}{35} \approx 1.1429\text{ A}$$ Both equivalent models yield identical physical results, confirming the theorem.

State and prove Maximum Power Transfer Theorem for a DC network. Derive the expression for maximum power and show that the transmission efficiency at maximum power is 50%. $$R_L = R_{th} \implies P_{\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}, \quad \eta = \frac{P_L}{P_{total}} \times 100\% = 50\%$$

Maximum Power Transfer Theorem: Maximum power is delivered to a load when $R_L = R_{th}$, resulting in $P_{\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}$. The transmission efficiency at maximum power transfer is strictly $50\%$, with the remaining $50\%$ dissipated as heat in internal resistance.

Maximum Power Transfer Theorem: Maximum power is delivered to a load when $R_L = R_{th}$, resulting in $P_{\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}$. The transmission efficiency at maximum power transfer is strictly $50\%$, with the remaining $50\%$ dissipated as heat in internal resistance.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Maximum Power Transfer Theorem is of paramount importance in communication systems, instrumentation, and audio engineering, where the primary objective is to maximize the transfer of energy from a signal source to a load rather than maximizing transmission efficiency.

Statement: The Maximum Power Transfer Theorem states that a linear bilateral active DC network delivers the maximum possible electrical power to a variable load resistance $R_L$ when the value of the load resistance is set exactly equal to the Thevenin equivalent resistance of the network as viewed from the load terminals: $$R_L = R_{th}$$

Mathematical Proof: Consider an active linear network replaced across its output terminals A-B by its Thevenin equivalent circuit comprising an open-circuit voltage $V_{th}$ and series internal resistance $R_{th}$. A variable load resistor $R_L$ is connected across terminals A-B. The loop current flowing through the circuit is given by Ohm's law: $$I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L}$$ The active power dissipated in the load resistor is: $$P_L = I_L^2 \cdot R_L = \left( \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} \right)^2 \cdot R_L = \frac{V_{th}^2 \cdot R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2}$$ To find the value of $R_L$ that maximizes $P_L$, we differentiate $P_L$ with respect to the independent variable $R_L$ and set the first derivative to zero: $$\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = 0$$ Applying the quotient rule of differential calculus, $\frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{u}{v}\right] = \frac{v \frac{du}{dx} - u \frac{dv}{dx}}{v^2}$: Let $u = V_{th}^2 \cdot R_L \implies \frac{du}{dR_L} = V_{th}^2$ Let $v = (R_{th} + R_L)^2 \implies \frac{dv}{dR_L} = 2(R_{th} + R_L)$ Substituting into the quotient derivative: $$\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = \frac{(R_{th} + R_L)^2 \cdot V_{th}^2 - V_{th}^2 \cdot R_L \cdot 2(R_{th} + R_L)}{(R_{th} + R_L)^4}$$ Factoring out the non-zero common term $V_{th}^2 (R_{th} + R_L)$: $$\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = \frac{V_{th}^2 (R_{th} + R_L) \left[ (R_{th} + R_L) - 2 R_L \right]}{(R_{th} + R_L)^4} = \frac{V_{th}^2 (R_{th} - R_L)}{(R_{th} + R_L)^3}$$ Setting $\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = 0$: $$\frac{V_{th}^2 (R_{th} - R_L)}{(R_{th} + R_L)^3} = 0 \implies R_{th} - R_L = 0 \implies R_L = R_{th}$$ To confirm that this condition corresponds to a mathematical maximum and not a minimum, we verify the second derivative $\frac{d^2 P_L}{dR_L^2}$ at $R_L = R_{th}$: $$\left. \frac{d^2 P_L}{dR_L^2} \right|_{R_L = R_{th}} = -\frac{V_{th}^2}{8 R_{th}^3} < 0$$ Because the second derivative is strictly negative, $R_L = R_{th}$ guarantees the global maximum power transfer.

Maximum Power Formula: Substituting the condition $R_L = R_{th}$ back into the power expression: $$P_{\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2 \cdot R_{th}}{(R_{th} + R_{th})^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2 \cdot R_{th}}{(2 R_{th})^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2 \cdot R_{th}}{4 R_{th}^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}$$

Efficiency Analysis: At the maximum power condition, the total power supplied by the source is: $$P_{total} = I_L^2 (R_{th} + R_L) = I_L^2 (2 R_{th}) = 2 P_{\max}$$ The electrical power transmission efficiency $\eta$ is: $$\eta = \frac{P_L}{P_{total}} \times 100\% = \frac{I_L^2 R_L}{I_L^2 (R_{th} + R_L)} \times 100\% = \frac{R_{th}}{2 R_{th}} \times 100\% = 50\%$$ This proves that under maximum power transfer, exactly half ($50\%$) of the total energy generated by the source is dissipated as heat inside the source's own internal resistance $R_{th}$, while only $50\%$ reaches the load. For this reason, power utility transmission systems never operate at maximum power transfer, but instead operate at $R_L \gg R_{th}$ to achieve high transmission efficiencies exceeding $90\%$.

Worked Numerical Problem: A DC source of $V_s = 60\text{ V}$ with internal resistance $R_1 = 12\,\Omega$ is connected across a bridge-T resistive network with series arm $R_2 = 8\,\Omega$ and parallel shunt arm $R_3 = 20\,\Omega$. Find the value of load resistance $R_L$ connected at the output terminals for maximum power transfer, and calculate the maximum power delivered. First, determine $V_{th}$: $$V_{th} = V_s \left( \frac{R_3}{R_1 + R_3} \right) = 60 \times \left( \frac{20}{12 + 20} \right) = 60 \times \frac{20}{32} = 37.5\text{ V}$$ Next, determine $R_{th}$ looking back from the output with $V_s$ short-circuited: $$R_{th} = R_2 + (R_1 \parallel R_3) = 8 + \frac{12 \times 20}{12 + 20} = 8 + \frac{240}{32} = 8 + 7.5 = 15.5\,\Omega$$ For maximum power transfer, the load resistance must be set to: $$R_L = R_{th} = 15.5\,\Omega$$ The maximum power delivered to $R_L$ is: $$P_{\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}} = \frac{(37.5)^2}{4 \times 15.5} = \frac{1406.25}{62} \approx 22.68\text{ W}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The Maximum Power Transfer Theorem is of paramount importance in communication systems, instrumentation, and audio engineering, where the primary objective is to maximize the transfer of energy from a signal source to a load rather than maximizing transmission efficiency.

Statement: The Maximum Power Transfer Theorem states that a linear bilateral active DC network delivers the maximum possible electrical power to a variable load resistance $R_L$ when the value of the load resistance is set exactly equal to the Thevenin equivalent resistance of the network as viewed from the load terminals: $$R_L = R_{th}$$

Mathematical Proof: Consider an active linear network replaced across its output terminals A-B by its Thevenin equivalent circuit comprising an open-circuit voltage $V_{th}$ and series internal resistance $R_{th}$. A variable load resistor $R_L$ is connected across terminals A-B. The loop current flowing through the circuit is given by Ohm's law: $$I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L}$$ The active power dissipated in the load resistor is: $$P_L = I_L^2 \cdot R_L = \left( \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} \right)^2 \cdot R_L = \frac{V_{th}^2 \cdot R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2}$$ To find the value of $R_L$ that maximizes $P_L$, we differentiate $P_L$ with respect to the independent variable $R_L$ and set the first derivative to zero: $$\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = 0$$ Applying the quotient rule of differential calculus, $\frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{u}{v}\right] = \frac{v \frac{du}{dx} - u \frac{dv}{dx}}{v^2}$: Let $u = V_{th}^2 \cdot R_L \implies \frac{du}{dR_L} = V_{th}^2$ Let $v = (R_{th} + R_L)^2 \implies \frac{dv}{dR_L} = 2(R_{th} + R_L)$ Substituting into the quotient derivative: $$\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = \frac{(R_{th} + R_L)^2 \cdot V_{th}^2 - V_{th}^2 \cdot R_L \cdot 2(R_{th} + R_L)}{(R_{th} + R_L)^4}$$ Factoring out the non-zero common term $V_{th}^2 (R_{th} + R_L)$: $$\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = \frac{V_{th}^2 (R_{th} + R_L) \left[ (R_{th} + R_L) - 2 R_L \right]}{(R_{th} + R_L)^4} = \frac{V_{th}^2 (R_{th} - R_L)}{(R_{th} + R_L)^3}$$ Setting $\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = 0$: $$\frac{V_{th}^2 (R_{th} - R_L)}{(R_{th} + R_L)^3} = 0 \implies R_{th} - R_L = 0 \implies R_L = R_{th}$$ To confirm that this condition corresponds to a mathematical maximum and not a minimum, we verify the second derivative $\frac{d^2 P_L}{dR_L^2}$ at $R_L = R_{th}$: $$\left. \frac{d^2 P_L}{dR_L^2} \right|_{R_L = R_{th}} = -\frac{V_{th}^2}{8 R_{th}^3} < 0$$ Because the second derivative is strictly negative, $R_L = R_{th}$ guarantees the global maximum power transfer.

Maximum Power Formula: Substituting the condition $R_L = R_{th}$ back into the power expression: $$P_{\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2 \cdot R_{th}}{(R_{th} + R_{th})^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2 \cdot R_{th}}{(2 R_{th})^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2 \cdot R_{th}}{4 R_{th}^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}$$

Efficiency Analysis: At the maximum power condition, the total power supplied by the source is: $$P_{total} = I_L^2 (R_{th} + R_L) = I_L^2 (2 R_{th}) = 2 P_{\max}$$ The electrical power transmission efficiency $\eta$ is: $$\eta = \frac{P_L}{P_{total}} \times 100\% = \frac{I_L^2 R_L}{I_L^2 (R_{th} + R_L)} \times 100\% = \frac{R_{th}}{2 R_{th}} \times 100\% = 50\%$$ This proves that under maximum power transfer, exactly half ($50\%$) of the total energy generated by the source is dissipated as heat inside the source's own internal resistance $R_{th}$, while only $50\%$ reaches the load. For this reason, power utility transmission systems never operate at maximum power transfer, but instead operate at $R_L \gg R_{th}$ to achieve high transmission efficiencies exceeding $90\%$.

Worked Numerical Problem: A DC source of $V_s = 60\text{ V}$ with internal resistance $R_1 = 12\,\Omega$ is connected across a bridge-T resistive network with series arm $R_2 = 8\,\Omega$ and parallel shunt arm $R_3 = 20\,\Omega$. Find the value of load resistance $R_L$ connected at the output terminals for maximum power transfer, and calculate the maximum power delivered. First, determine $V_{th}$: $$V_{th} = V_s \left( \frac{R_3}{R_1 + R_3} \right) = 60 \times \left( \frac{20}{12 + 20} \right) = 60 \times \frac{20}{32} = 37.5\text{ V}$$ Next, determine $R_{th}$ looking back from the output with $V_s$ short-circuited: $$R_{th} = R_2 + (R_1 \parallel R_3) = 8 + \frac{12 \times 20}{12 + 20} = 8 + \frac{240}{32} = 8 + 7.5 = 15.5\,\Omega$$ For maximum power transfer, the load resistance must be set to: $$R_L = R_{th} = 15.5\,\Omega$$ The maximum power delivered to $R_L$ is: $$P_{\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}} = \frac{(37.5)^2}{4 \times 15.5} = \frac{1406.25}{62} \approx 22.68\text{ W}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The Maximum Power Transfer Theorem is of paramount importance in communication systems, instrumentation, and audio engineering, where the primary objective is to maximize the transfer of energy from a signal source to a load rather than maximizing transmission efficiency.

Mathematical Proof: Consider an active linear network replaced across its output terminals A-B by its Thevenin equivalent circuit comprising an open-circuit voltage $V_{th}$ and series internal resistance $R_{th}$. For this reason, power utility transmission systems never operate at maximum power transfer, but instead operate at $R_L \gg R_{th}$ to achieve high transmission efficiencies exceeding $90\%$.

Worked Numerical Problem: A DC source of $V_s = 60\text{ V}$ with internal resistance $R_1 = 12\,\Omega$ is connected across a bridge-T resistive network with series arm $R_2 = 8\,\Omega$ and parallel shunt arm $R_3 = 20\,\Omega$. Find the value of load resistance $R_L$ connected at the output terminals for maximum power transfer, and calculate the maximum power delivered. AC Fundamentals & Magnetic Circuits

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Short Answer ID: BEEE-Q6

Explain the generation of sinusoidal alternating voltage by a coil rotating in a uniform magnetic field. Define instantaneous value, amplitude, time period, frequency, and phasor representation. Explain the generation of sinusoidal alternating voltage by a coil rotating in a uniform magnetic field. Define instantaneous value, amplitude, time period, frequency, and phasor representation. Explain the generation of sinusoidal alternating voltage by a coil rotating in a uniform magnetic field. Define instantaneous value, amplitude, time period, frequency, and phasor representation.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$v(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t \pm \phi), \quad \omega = 2\pi f = \frac{2\pi}{T}, \quad \mathbf{V} = V_{rms}\angle \phi$$

Sinusoidal AC Generation: Derived from Faraday’s law $e(t) = -N \frac{d\Phi}{dt} = N B A \omega \sin(\omega t)$ for a coil rotating in a uniform magnetic field.
Phasor: Directed vector rotating counterclockwise at angular velocity $\omega$, representing RMS magnitude and phase angle in the complex plane.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$v(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t \pm \phi), \quad \omega = 2\pi f = \frac{2\pi}{T}, \quad \mathbf{V} = V_{rms}\angle \phi$$

Sinusoidal AC Generation: Derived from Faraday’s law $e(t) = -N \frac{d\Phi}{dt} = N B A \omega \sin(\omega t)$ for a coil rotating in a uniform magnetic field.
Phasor: Directed vector rotating counterclockwise at angular velocity $\omega$, representing RMS magnitude and phase angle in the complex plane.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Sinusoidal alternating voltage is generated fundamentally on the principle of Faraday's law of electromagnetic induction. When a planar rectangular conductor loop of $N$ turns and cross-sectional area $A$ rotates at a uniform angular velocity $\omega$ radians per second within a uniform, stationary magnetic field of flux density $B$ Teslas, the magnetic flux linkage through the coil varies continuously as a function of time.

At any instant of time $t$, let $\theta = \omega t$ denote the angle between the normal vector to the coil plane and the magnetic field vector. The instantaneous magnetic flux linked with the coil is: $$\Phi(t) = B \cdot A \cos(\omega t)$$ According to Faraday's law, the electromotive force (EMF) induced across the coil terminals is equal to the negative rate of change of magnetic flux linkages: $$e(t) = -N \frac{d\Phi}{dt} = -N \frac{d}{dt}\left[ B A \cos(\omega t) \right] = N B A \omega \sin(\omega t)$$ Defining the peak or maximum induced voltage as $V_m = N B A \omega$, the standard mathematical equation for an alternating sinusoidal voltage is: $$v(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t)$$

The fundamental parameters governing this alternating waveform are defined as follows: 1. Instantaneous Value ($v$ or $i$): The magnitude of voltage or current measured at any specific time instant $t$, expressed as $v(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t \pm \phi)$. 2. Amplitude / Peak Value ($V_m$ or $I_m$): The maximum instantaneous value attained by the waveform during either the positive or negative half-cycle. The total excursion between positive and negative peaks is the Peak-to-Peak value ($V_{p-p} = 2 V_m$). 3. Cycle: One complete sequence of positive and negative variations of the alternating quantity, repeated continuously at uniform intervals. 4. Time Period ($T$): The duration in seconds required by the waveform to complete one full cycle. In terms of angular velocity: $$T = \frac{2\pi}{\omega}\text{ seconds}$$ 5. Frequency ($f$): The number of complete cycles executed per second, measured in Hertz ($\text{Hz}$): $$f = \frac{1}{T} = \frac{\omega}{2\pi} \iff \omega = 2\pi f\text{ rad/s}$$ 6. Phase and Phase Difference: Phase defines the fractional part of a period that has elapsed relative to an arbitrary reference origin, expressed as an angle $\phi$. If two sinusoidal quantities of the same frequency are given by $v_1(t) = V_{m1}\sin(\omega t)$ and $v_2(t) = V_{m2}\sin(\omega t - \phi)$, $v_2$ is said to lag $v_1$ by phase angle $\phi$ (or $v_1$ leads $v_2$ by $\phi$).

Phasor Representation: Because analyzing sinusoidal waveforms in the time domain involves cumbersome trigonometric identities, alternating quantities are represented in electrical engineering by Phasors. A phasor is a directed line segment (vector) of length proportional to the RMS value (or peak value) of the sinusoidal wave, rotating counterclockwise in the complex plane at angular frequency $\omega$. The vertical projection of this rotating vector onto the imaginary axis at any instant $t$ precisely reproduces the instantaneous value $V_m \sin(\omega t + \phi)$. In complex notation, a sinusoidal voltage $v(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t + \phi)$ is expressed in polar form as $\mathbf{V} = V_{\text{rms}} \angle \phi$ or in rectangular form as $\mathbf{V} = V_{\text{rms}}(\cos\phi + j\sin\phi)$, enabling AC network analysis via basic complex algebra.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: BEEE-Q7

Derive the mathematical expressions for the RMS value, Average value, Form Factor, and Peak Factor of a pure sinusoidal alternating voltage waveform. Derive the mathematical expressions for the RMS value, Average value, Form Factor, and Peak Factor of a pure sinusoidal alternating voltage waveform. Derive the mathematical expressions for the RMS value, Average value, Form Factor, and Peak Factor of a pure sinusoidal alternating voltage waveform.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}} \approx 0.707 V_m, \quad V_{avg} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi} \approx 0.637 V_m, \quad K_f = \frac{V_{rms}}{V_{avg}} = 1.11, \quad K_p = \frac{V_m}{V_{rms}} = \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414$$

RMS Value: Equivalent DC heating value ($V_m / \sqrt{2}$).
Average Value: Half-cycle arithmetic mean ($2 V_m / \pi$).
Form Factor: $K_f = 1.11$ for pure sine wave.
Peak Factor: $K_p = 1.414$, governing dielectric insulation withstand limits.

⚡ Key Equation

$$V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}} \approx 0.707 V_m, \quad V_{avg} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi} \approx 0.637 V_m, \quad K_f = \frac{V_{rms}}{V_{avg}} = 1.11, \quad K_p = \frac{V_m}{V_{rms}} = \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}} \approx 0.707 V_m, \quad V_{avg} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi} \approx 0.637 V_m, \quad K_f = \frac{V_{rms}}{V_{avg}} = 1.11, \quad K_p = \frac{V_m}{V_{rms}} = \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414$$

RMS Value: Equivalent DC heating value ($V_m / \sqrt{2}$).
Average Value: Half-cycle arithmetic mean ($2 V_m / \pi$).
Form Factor: $K_f = 1.11$ for pure sine wave.
Peak Factor: $K_p = 1.414$, governing dielectric insulation withstand limits.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The quantitative assessment of alternating voltages and currents requires standard scalar metrics that relate time-varying quantities to equivalent steady DC levels.

1. Average Value ($V_{avg}$): The average value of an alternating quantity over a time interval $T$ is the arithmetic mean of all instantaneous values over that period: $$V_{avg} = \frac{1}{T} \int_{0}^{T} v(t)\,dt$$ For a symmetrical sinusoidal waveform $v(t) = V_m \sin\theta$ (where $\theta = \omega t$), the positive and negative half-cycles are identical in shape but opposite in sign. Integrating over a full period ($0$ to $2\pi$) yields identically zero. Therefore, by international convention, the average value of a symmetrical AC wave is evaluated strictly over one half-cycle ($0$ to $\pi$): $$V_{avg} = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_{0}^{\pi} V_m \sin\theta\,d\theta = \frac{V_m}{\pi} \left[ -\cos\theta \right]_0^\pi$$ Evaluating the limits: $$V_{avg} = \frac{V_m}{\pi} \left[ -\cos(\pi) - (-\cos(0)) \right] = \frac{V_m}{\pi} \left[ -(-1) - (-1) \right] = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [1 + 1] = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}$$ Substituting $\pi \approx 3.14159$: $$V_{avg} = \frac{2}{\pi} V_m \approx 0.6366 V_m$$

2. Root Mean Square (RMS) / Effective Value ($V_{rms}$): The RMS or effective value of an alternating current is defined on the basis of heating effect (Joule's law): it is that steady direct current (DC) which, when flowing through a given resistance for a specified time, produces exactly the same amount of heat energy as produced by the alternating current flowing through the same resistance for the same duration. Heat produced by an instantaneous current $i(t)$ in resistance $R$ over an infinitesimal duration $dt$ is $dH = i^2(t) R dt$. Over one full cycle of period $T$: $$H_{AC} = \int_{0}^{T} i^2(t) R\,dt$$ The heat produced by an equivalent direct current $I_{rms}$ over the same period is: $$H_{DC} = I_{rms}^2 R T$$ Equating both expressions: $$I_{rms}^2 R T = R \int_{0}^{T} i^2(t)\,dt \implies I_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T} i^2(t)\,dt}$$ Applying this definition to a sinusoidal voltage $v(\theta) = V_m \sin\theta$ over period $T = 2\pi$: $$V_{rms}^2 = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{0}^{2\pi} V_m^2 \sin^2\theta\,d\theta$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\sin^2\theta = \frac{1 - \cos(2\theta)}{2}$: $$V_{rms}^2 = \frac{V_m^2}{2\pi} \int_{0}^{2\pi} \frac{1 - \cos(2\theta)}{2}\,d\theta = \frac{V_m^2}{4\pi} \left[ \int_{0}^{2\pi} 1\,d\theta - \int_{0}^{2\pi} \cos(2\theta)\,d\theta \right]$$ The integral of $\cos(2\theta)$ over its full period of $2\pi$ is zero: $$V_{rms}^2 = \frac{V_m^2}{4\pi} \left[ \theta \right]_0^{2\pi} - 0 = \frac{V_m^2}{4\pi} [2\pi - 0] = \frac{V_m^2}{4\pi} \cdot 2\pi = \frac{V_m^2}{2}$$ Taking the square root: $$V_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{V_m^2}{2}} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}} \approx 0.7071 V_m$$

3. Form Factor ($K_f$): The form factor is defined as the mathematical ratio of the RMS value to the half-cycle average value of the alternating waveform: $$K_f = \frac{V_{rms}}{V_{avg}} = \frac{\frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}}}{\frac{2 V_m}{\pi}} = \frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2}} = \frac{3.14159}{2 \times 1.4142} = \frac{3.14159}{2.8284} \approx 1.11$$ The value $1.11$ is the universal form factor for pure sinusoidal waves, appearing prominently in transformer EMF equations.

4. Peak Factor / Crest Factor ($K_p$): The peak factor is defined as the ratio of the peak (maximum) value to the RMS value: $$K_p = \frac{V_m}{V_{rms}} = \frac{V_m}{\frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}}} = \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414$$ The peak factor is vital for insulation design, specifying the dielectric breakdown voltage required to withstand peak stresses for a given nominal RMS operating voltage.

3. Form Factor ($K_f$): The form factor is defined as the mathematical ratio of the RMS value to the half-cycle average value of the alternating waveform: $$K_f = \frac{V_{rms}}{V_{avg}} = \frac{\frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}}}{\frac{2 V_m}{\pi}} = \frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2}} = \frac{3.14159}{2 \times 1.4142} = \frac{3.14159}{2.8284} \approx 1.11$$ The value $1.11$ is the universal form factor for pure sinusoidal waves, appearing prominently in transformer EMF equations.

4. Peak Factor / Crest Factor ($K_p$): The peak factor is defined as the ratio of the peak (maximum) value to the RMS value: $$K_p = \frac{V_m}{V_{rms}} = \frac{V_m}{\frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}}} = \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414$$ The peak factor is vital for insulation design, specifying the dielectric breakdown voltage required to withstand peak stresses for a given nominal RMS operating voltage.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Numerical Problem ID: BEEE-Q8

Derive the expressions for impedance, current, phase angle, and power in a series R-L and series R-C circuit excited by sinusoidal AC. Explain the impedance and power triangles with a numerical problem. Derive the expressions for impedance, current, phase angle, and power in a series R-L and series R-C circuit excited by sinusoidal AC. Explain the impedance and power triangles with a numerical problem. Derive the expressions for impedance, current, phase angle, and power in a series R-L and series R-C circuit excited by sinusoidal AC. Explain the impedance and power triangles with a numerical problem.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathbf{Z} = R + j(X_L - X_C), \quad P = V I \cos\phi, \quad Q = V I \sin\phi, \quad S = V I = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2}, \quad pf = \cos\phi = \frac{R}{|\mathbf{Z}|}$$

Series R-L: Current lags voltage by $\phi = \tan^{-1}(X_L/R)$, lagging power factor.
Series R-C: Current leads voltage by $\phi = \tan^{-1}(X_C/R)$, leading power factor.
Power Triangle: Real active power $P$ (Watts), reactive power $Q$ (VAR), apparent power $S$ (VA).

$$\mathbf{Z} = R + j(X_L - X_C), \quad P = V I \cos\phi, \quad Q = V I \sin\phi, \quad S = V I = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2}, \quad pf = \cos\phi = \frac{R}{|\mathbf{Z}|}$$

$$\mathbf{Z} = R + j(X_L - X_C), \quad P = V I \cos\phi, \quad Q = V I \sin\phi, \quad S = V I = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2}, \quad pf = \cos\phi = \frac{R}{|\mathbf{Z}|}$$

Series R-L: Current lags voltage by $\phi = \tan^{-1}(X_L/R)$, lagging power factor.
Series R-C: Current leads voltage by $\phi = \tan^{-1}(X_C/R)$, leading power factor.
Power Triangle: Real active power $P$ (Watts), reactive power $Q$ (VAR), apparent power $S$ (VA).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

When linear passive elements are excited by a steady-state sinusoidal voltage $v(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t)$, their dynamic responses depend on the nature of their energy storage mechanisms.

In a Series R-L Circuit comprising a resistor $R$ and inductor $L$ carrying common loop current $i(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t)$: The voltage across the resistor is in phase with the current: $\mathbf{V}_R = I R \angle 0^\circ$. The voltage across the ideal inductor leads the current by $90^\circ$ due to Lenz's law: $\mathbf{V}_L = I X_L \angle 90^\circ = j I X_L$, where inductive reactance is $X_L = \omega L = 2\pi f L\,\Omega$. By Kirchhoff's voltage law, the total supply phasor voltage is: $$\mathbf{V} = \mathbf{V}_R + \mathbf{V}_L = I R + j I X_L = I (R + j X_L) = I \mathbf{Z}$$ The complex impedance is $\mathbf{Z} = R + j X_L$, with magnitude and phase angle: $$|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{R^2 + X_L^2}, \quad \phi = \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{X_L}{R} \right)$$ Because $\mathbf{V}$ leads $\mathbf{I}$ by $\phi$, the current lags the supply voltage by phase angle $\phi$.

In a Series R-C Circuit comprising a resistor $R$ and capacitor $C$: The capacitive reactance is $X_C = \frac{1}{\omega C} = \frac{1}{2\pi f C}\,\Omega$. The voltage across the capacitor lags the current by $90^\circ$: $\mathbf{V}_C = -j I X_C = I X_C \angle -90^\circ$. The supply voltage is: $$\mathbf{V} = \mathbf{V}_R + \mathbf{V}_C = I(R - j X_C) = I \mathbf{Z}$$ The complex impedance is $\mathbf{Z} = R - j X_C$, with magnitude $|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{R^2 + X_C^2}$ and phase angle $\phi = -\tan^{-1}\left( \frac{X_C}{R} \right)$. In an R-C circuit, current leads the supply voltage by phase angle $|\phi|$.

Power Definitions and Power Triangle: 1. Real / Active Power ($P$): The net rate of usable energy consumed by the resistive element, measured in Watts ($\text{W}$): $$P = V_{rms} I_{rms} \cos\phi = I_{rms}^2 R$$ 2. Reactive Power ($Q$): The peak rate of energy oscillating back and forth between the magnetic/electric fields and the source, doing no net work, measured in Volt-Amperes Reactive ($\text{VAR}$): $$Q = V_{rms} I_{rms} \sin\phi = I_{rms}^2 X$$ 3. Apparent Power ($S$): The total complex power capacity supplied by the source, measured in Volt-Amperes ($\text{VA}$): $$S = V_{rms} I_{rms} = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2}$$ 4. Power Factor ($pf$): The cosine of the phase angle between voltage and current: $$pf = \cos\phi = \frac{P}{S} = \frac{R}{|\mathbf{Z}|}$$ Power factor is lagging for inductive circuits and leading for capacitive circuits.

Complete Worked Numerical Problem: A non-inductive resistor $R = 30\,\Omega$ is connected in series with an inductive coil of inductance $L = 0.1273\text{ H}$ across a $230\text{ V}, 50\text{ Hz}$ single-phase AC supply. Calculate: (i) Inductive reactance, (ii) Total circuit impedance, (iii) RMS current, (iv) Power factor, (v) Active power, (vi) Reactive power, and (vii) Apparent power.

Step 1: Inductive reactance: $$X_L = 2\pi f L = 2 \times 3.14159 \times 50 \times 0.1273 = 100 \times 3.14159 \times 0.1273 = 40.0\,\Omega$$

Step 2: Total impedance: $$\mathbf{Z} = R + j X_L = 30 + j 40\,\Omega$$ $$|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{R^2 + X_L^2} = \sqrt{30^2 + 40^2} = \sqrt{900 + 1600} = \sqrt{2500} = 50.0\,\Omega$$ $$\phi = \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{X_L}{R} \right) = \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{40}{30} \right) = \tan^{-1}(1.333) \approx 53.13^\circ$$

Step 3: RMS circuit current: $$I = \frac{V}{|\mathbf{Z}|} = \frac{230}{50} = 4.60\text{ A}$$

Step 4: Power factor: $$pf = \cos\phi = \cos(53.13^\circ) = \frac{R}{|\mathbf{Z}|} = \frac{30}{50} = 0.60\text{ lagging}$$

Step 5: Active power dissipated: $$P = V I \cos\phi = 230 \times 4.60 \times 0.60 = 634.8\text{ W}$$ Alternatively: $P = I^2 R = (4.60)^2 \times 30 = 21.16 \times 30 = 634.8\text{ W}$.

Step 6: Reactive power: $$\sin\phi = \sin(53.13^\circ) = 0.80$$ $$Q = V I \sin\phi = 230 \times 4.60 \times 0.80 = 846.4\text{ VAR}$$

Step 7: Apparent power: $$S = V I = 230 \times 4.60 = 1058.0\text{ VA}$$ Verification: $\sqrt{P^2 + Q^2} = \sqrt{634.8^2 + 846.4^2} = \sqrt{402971 + 716393} = \sqrt{1119364} = 1058.0\text{ VA}$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

When linear passive elements are excited by a steady-state sinusoidal voltage $v(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t)$, their dynamic responses depend on the nature of their energy storage mechanisms.

In a Series R-L Circuit comprising a resistor $R$ and inductor $L$ carrying common loop current $i(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t)$: The voltage across the resistor is in phase with the current: $\mathbf{V}_R = I R \angle 0^\circ$. The voltage across the ideal inductor leads the current by $90^\circ$ due to Lenz's law: $\mathbf{V}_L = I X_L \angle 90^\circ = j I X_L$, where inductive reactance is $X_L = \omega L = 2\pi f L\,\Omega$. By Kirchhoff's voltage law, the total supply phasor voltage is: $$\mathbf{V} = \mathbf{V}_R + \mathbf{V}_L = I R + j I X_L = I (R + j X_L) = I \mathbf{Z}$$ The complex impedance is $\mathbf{Z} = R + j X_L$, with magnitude and phase angle: $$|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{R^2 + X_L^2}, \quad \phi = \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{X_L}{R} \right)$$ Because $\mathbf{V}$ leads $\mathbf{I}$ by $\phi$, the current lags the supply voltage by phase angle $\phi$.

In a Series R-C Circuit comprising a resistor $R$ and capacitor $C$: The capacitive reactance is $X_C = \frac{1}{\omega C} = \frac{1}{2\pi f C}\,\Omega$. The voltage across the capacitor lags the current by $90^\circ$: $\mathbf{V}_C = -j I X_C = I X_C \angle -90^\circ$. The supply voltage is: $$\mathbf{V} = \mathbf{V}_R + \mathbf{V}_C = I(R - j X_C) = I \mathbf{Z}$$ The complex impedance is $\mathbf{Z} = R - j X_C$, with magnitude $|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{R^2 + X_C^2}$ and phase angle $\phi = -\tan^{-1}\left( \frac{X_C}{R} \right)$. In an R-C circuit, current leads the supply voltage by phase angle $|\phi|$.

Power Definitions and Power Triangle: 1. Real / Active Power ($P$): The net rate of usable energy consumed by the resistive element, measured in Watts ($\text{W}$): $$P = V_{rms} I_{rms} \cos\phi = I_{rms}^2 R$$ 2. Reactive Power ($Q$): The peak rate of energy oscillating back and forth between the magnetic/electric fields and the source, doing no net work, measured in Volt-Amperes Reactive ($\text{VAR}$): $$Q = V_{rms} I_{rms} \sin\phi = I_{rms}^2 X$$ 3. Apparent Power ($S$): The total complex power capacity supplied by the source, measured in Volt-Amperes ($\text{VA}$): $$S = V_{rms} I_{rms} = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2}$$ 4. Power Factor ($pf$): The cosine of the phase angle between voltage and current: $$pf = \cos\phi = \frac{P}{S} = \frac{R}{|\mathbf{Z}|}$$ Power factor is lagging for inductive circuits and leading for capacitive circuits.

Complete Worked Numerical Problem: A non-inductive resistor $R = 30\,\Omega$ is connected in series with an inductive coil of inductance $L = 0.1273\text{ H}$ across a $230\text{ V}, 50\text{ Hz}$ single-phase AC supply. Calculate: (i) Inductive reactance, (ii) Total circuit impedance, (iii) RMS current, (iv) Power factor, (v) Active power, (vi) Reactive power, and (vii) Apparent power.

Step 1: Inductive reactance: $$X_L = 2\pi f L = 2 \times 3.14159 \times 50 \times 0.1273 = 100 \times 3.14159 \times 0.1273 = 40.0\,\Omega$$

Step 2: Total impedance: $$\mathbf{Z} = R + j X_L = 30 + j 40\,\Omega$$ $$|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{R^2 + X_L^2} = \sqrt{30^2 + 40^2} = \sqrt{900 + 1600} = \sqrt{2500} = 50.0\,\Omega$$ $$\phi = \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{X_L}{R} \right) = \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{40}{30} \right) = \tan^{-1}(1.333) \approx 53.13^\circ$$

Step 3: RMS circuit current: $$I = \frac{V}{|\mathbf{Z}|} = \frac{230}{50} = 4.60\text{ A}$$

Step 4: Power factor: $$pf = \cos\phi = \cos(53.13^\circ) = \frac{R}{|\mathbf{Z}|} = \frac{30}{50} = 0.60\text{ lagging}$$

Step 5: Active power dissipated: $$P = V I \cos\phi = 230 \times 4.60 \times 0.60 = 634.8\text{ W}$$ Alternatively: $P = I^2 R = (4.60)^2 \times 30 = 21.16 \times 30 = 634.8\text{ W}$.

Step 6: Reactive power: $$\sin\phi = \sin(53.13^\circ) = 0.80$$ $$Q = V I \sin\phi = 230 \times 4.60 \times 0.80 = 846.4\text{ VAR}$$

Step 7: Apparent power: $$S = V I = 230 \times 4.60 = 1058.0\text{ VA}$$ Verification: $\sqrt{P^2 + Q^2} = \sqrt{634.8^2 + 846.4^2} = \sqrt{402971 + 716393} = \sqrt{1119364} = 1058.0\text{ VA}$.

Long Derivation ID: BEEE-Q9

Derive the condition and expression for resonant frequency in a series R-L-C circuit. Derive the expressions for Quality factor (Q) and half-power bandwidth (BW). Derive the condition and expression for resonant frequency in a series R-L-C circuit. Derive the expressions for Quality factor (Q) and half-power bandwidth (BW). Derive the condition and expression for resonant frequency in a series R-L-C circuit. Derive the expressions for Quality factor (Q) and half-power bandwidth (BW).

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$f_r = \frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{LC}}, \quad |\mathbf{Z}_r| = R, \quad I_0 = \frac{V}{R}, \quad Q = \frac{1}{R}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}, \quad BW = f_2 - f_1 = \frac{f_r}{Q} = \frac{R}{2\pi L}$$

Series Resonance: Occurs when $X_L = X_C$, giving purely resistive minimum impedance $|\mathbf{Z}| = R$, maximum in-phase current ($pf = 1.0$), and voltage magnification across $L$ and $C$ ($V_L = V_C = Q \cdot V$). Narrow bandwidth indicates high selectivity.

Series Resonance: Occurs when $X_L = X_C$, giving purely resistive minimum impedance $|\mathbf{Z}| = R$, maximum in-phase current ($pf = 1.0$), and voltage magnification across $L$ and $C$ ($V_L = V_C = Q \cdot V$). Narrow bandwidth indicates high selectivity.

$$f_r = \frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{LC}}, \quad |\mathbf{Z}_r| = R, \quad I_0 = \frac{V}{R}, \quad Q = \frac{1}{R}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}, \quad BW = f_2 - f_1 = \frac{f_r}{Q} = \frac{R}{2\pi L}$$

Series Resonance: Occurs when $X_L = X_C$, giving purely resistive minimum impedance $|\mathbf{Z}| = R$, maximum in-phase current ($pf = 1.0$), and voltage magnification across $L$ and $C$ ($V_L = V_C = Q \cdot V$). Narrow bandwidth indicates high selectivity.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A series R-L-C circuit consists of a resistor $R$, an inductor $L$, and a capacitor $C$ connected in series across an alternating sinusoidal voltage source $\mathbf{V} = V \angle 0^\circ$. The total complex impedance of the circuit as a function of angular frequency $\omega$ is: $$\mathbf{Z}(\omega) = R + j\left( \omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} \right) = R + j X_{net}$$ The net reactance of the circuit is $X_{net} = X_L - X_C = \omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C}$.

Condition for Series Resonance: Electrical resonance occurs when the supply frequency is tuned such that the inductive reactance exactly balances and cancels the capacitive reactance: $$X_L = X_C \implies \omega L = \frac{1}{\omega C}$$ At this specific frequency, denoted as the resonant angular frequency $\omega_r$: $$\omega_r^2 = \frac{1}{L C} \implies \omega_r = \frac{1}{\sqrt{L C}}\text{ rad/s}$$ The resonant frequency in Hertz ($f_r$) is: $$f_r = \frac{\omega_r}{2\pi} = \frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{L C}}\text{ Hz}$$

Salient Characteristics at Series Resonance: 1. Minimum Impedance: The net reactive component vanishes ($X_{net} = 0$). The circuit impedance is purely resistive and reaches its absolute minimum: $$\mathbf{Z}_r = R + j 0 = R$$ 2. Maximum Current: Because impedance is minimal, the loop current reaches its maximum possible amplitude and is strictly in phase with the supply voltage: $$\mathbf{I}_{0} = \frac{\mathbf{V}}{R} \angle 0^\circ$$ 3. Unity Power Factor: The phase angle is $\phi = 0^\circ$, meaning the power factor is $\cos(0^\circ) = 1.0$. 4. Voltage Magnification: While the net reactive voltage is zero ($\mathbf{V}_L + \mathbf{V}_C = 0$), the individual voltages across the inductor and capacitor can be enormously higher than the supply voltage: $$V_L = I_0 X_L = \left(\frac{V}{R}\right) \omega_r L = Q \cdot V$$ $$V_C = I_0 X_C = \left(\frac{V}{R}\right) \frac{1}{\omega_r C} = Q \cdot V$$ where $Q$ is the circuit Quality Factor. For this reason, series resonance is also termed Voltage Resonance.

Quality Factor ($Q$-Factor): The Quality Factor ($Q$) quantifies the sharpness of resonance and frequency selectivity. It is defined fundamentally as $2\pi$ times the ratio of maximum energy stored in reactive components to the energy dissipated per cycle in the resistance: $$Q = 2\pi \frac{\text{Maximum Energy Stored}}{\text{Energy Dissipated per Cycle}} = \frac{\omega_r L}{R} = \frac{1}{\omega_r C R}$$ Substituting $\omega_r = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}$: $$Q = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} \frac{L}{R} = \frac{1}{R} \sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}$$

Half-Power Frequencies and Bandwidth ($BW$): The half-power frequencies $\omega_1$ (lower cutoff) and $\omega_2$ (upper cutoff) are the operating frequencies at which the circuit current drops to $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$ ($70.7\%$) of its peak resonant value $I_0$, causing power dissipation to drop to exactly half ($P = I^2 R = \frac{1}{2} I_0^2 R$). At these frequencies: $$|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{R^2 + X_{net}^2} = \sqrt{2} R \implies R^2 + X_{net}^2 = 2 R^2 \implies X_{net} = \pm R$$ $$\omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} = \pm R$$ Solving this quadratic equation yields: $$\omega_1 = -\frac{R}{2L} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{R}{2L}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{LC}}, \quad \omega_2 = +\frac{R}{2L} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{R}{2L}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{LC}}$$ The resonant frequency is the geometric mean of the cutoff frequencies: $\omega_r = \sqrt{\omega_1 \omega_2}$. The Bandwidth ($BW$ or $\Delta \omega$) is the frequency span between the upper and lower half-power frequencies: $$\Delta \omega = \omega_2 - \omega_1 = \frac{R}{L}\text{ rad/s}$$ In Hertz: $$BW = f_2 - f_1 = \frac{R}{2\pi L}\text{ Hz}$$ Dividing both sides by $f_r$: $$\frac{BW}{f_r} = \frac{R / (2\pi L)}{\omega_r / (2\pi)} = \frac{R}{\omega_r L} = \frac{1}{Q} \implies BW = \frac{f_r}{Q}$$ This fundamental relationship proves that a high-Q circuit possesses a narrow bandwidth and superior selectivity, which is indispensable for tuning radio receivers and filtering out adjacent interfering communication channels.
